Hi, yeah… I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to read or mark. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document fully intending to get through it, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.

Yeah, I mean… it’s most obvious with work stuff because that’s where it causes problems, but it’s kind of the same pattern elsewhere too, I think. Yeah, it does. I mean, home is where it gets a bit… embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not just me losing focus on some boring article or whatever.

I don’t know, actually. I mean, I worry about things when there’s something obvious going on, like if I’ve forgotten something or there’s a deadline — but just worrying for no reason… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.

I guess I haven’t really noticed worry as the main thing. It’s not what I’d say I’m dealing with most, anyway.

But no, I wouldn’t say I have this strong need to be moving all the time. At least, it’s not something I’ve noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, actually. Sorry, that’s a bit of a ramble.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed worry as the main thing. I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel a bit flat or fed up, but it usually feels tied to being behind on things or just being tired.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think “zone out” is probably the right phrase, though I don’t know if I’d call it detached exactly.

It’s more like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or some curriculum document I genuinely mean to get through, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been looking at the same page the whole time. My eyes are on it, but I’m thinking about three other things, or sort of drifting, and I haven’t actually taken in what’s in front of me.

It’s not like I’m unaware of where I am, really. It’s just… I lose the thread very easily, and then I’m annoyed because I did sit down to do it. That’s actually a big part of why I came in.

Not as the main thing, anyway.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you ever feel detached or as if you 'zone out' and lose track of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “zone out” is probably the right phrase, though I don’t know if I’d call it detached exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or some curriculum document I genuinely mean to get through, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been looking at the same page the whole time. I mean, I have days where I feel a bit flat or fed up, but it usually feels tied to being behind on things or just being tired.\n\nActually, “down” is hard to judge, because sometimes I’ll come home from school and just sort of collapse on the sofa, and then I realize I’ve still got marking or laundry or whatever, and that makes me feel worse — but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just… normal life piling up.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down a lot recently. Not as the main thing, anyway.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you ever feel detached or as if you 'zone out' and lose track of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “zone out” is probably the right phrase, though I don’t know if I’d call it detached exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or some curriculum document I genuinely mean to get through, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been looking at the same page the whole time. My eyes are on it, but I’m thinking about three other things, or sort of drifting, and I haven’t actually taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m unaware of where I am, really. It’s just… I lose the thread very easily, and then I’m annoyed because I did sit down to do it. That’s actually a big part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose or misplace things you need, like keys, papers, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. I’m constantly hunting for things I need for the day — classroom keys, lesson notes, my USB drive, that sort of thing.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’ll be sure I had something in my hand five minutes earlier, and then suddenly it’s just gone. And then I’m retracing my steps around the staff room or my classroom, checking the same pile of papers three times.\n\nIt happens with refereeing stuff as well — like the whistle or cards — and I’ll think, “Right, I definitely put them somewhere sensible,” which apparently means absolutely nothing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these attention and organization problems been fairly constant throughout your life, rather than only starting during a particular period?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. It didn’t suddenly start recently or anything.

I mean, even in primary school, my reports had that sort of line teachers love — “bright but careless,” “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised,” things like that. My mum still brings it up sometimes, usually when she’s making some point about me leaving a cupboard open or— sorry, that’s not really the question.

But yes, it goes back a long way. My parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So it’s pretty familiar, unfortunately.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah… I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things I need to read or mark. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document fully intending to get through it, and then maybe ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nIt’s not even that I don’t care — I’m a teacher, so obviously I need to do it properly — but my mind’s off thinking about three other things, and I’ve barely absorbed anything in front of me. It’s frustrating, because I’ll have been “working” for a while, but not actually getting anywhere.\n\nDoctor: Did you have the same kind of trouble focusing when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I didn’t think of it that way at the time, obviously — you’re just a kid, and you think everyone’s being dramatic about homework — but my primary school reports had a lot of that “bright but careless” kind of wording.\n\nThere was one teacher who wrote that I didn’t finish work unless someone was basically watching me, which my mum still brings up sometimes. And my parents were forever pulling these crumpled worksheets out of my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, but just never handed in. My bag was a disaster, actually — old lunch notes, football stickers, random permission slips… but yeah, the schoolwork thing was definitely there even then.\n\nDoctor: Do you have this same kind of trouble staying focused in other parts of your life too, not just when you’re reading or marking for work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s most obvious with work stuff because that’s where it causes problems, but it’s kind of the same pattern elsewhere too, I think. Like I’ll sit down properly intending to do something, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been looking at the same page or the same bit of whatever, and my head’s off somewhere else entirely.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not choosing to drift off — I’ll be thinking about three other things and then suddenly notice I haven’t really taken in what I was meant to be doing. With marking it’s just easier to point to, because there’s this pile of essays sitting there making me feel guilty.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this difficulty concentrating cause problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, home is where it gets a bit… embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not just me losing focus on some boring article or whatever.\n\nMy wife’s started taking over things like bills and reminders, because she says she can’t really count on me to keep track of them. Which sounds harsh, but she’s not wrong. I’ll fully intend to sort something, and then it just sort of slips, and then suddenly it’s overdue or she’s had to remind me three times.\n\nAnd work-wise it’s got more serious than I wanted to admit — my department chair actually put me on a support plan because I wasn’t following through on things reliably enough. I remember sitting in that meeting thinking about how the room had those awful flickering lights, which is not the point, obviously… but yeah. It’s causing real problems, not just annoying me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing something you find genuinely interesting, are you usually able to stay focused?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, if it really grabs me I can do better… but it’s not reliable, which is kind of why I came in.\n\nLike, with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, I’ll sit down fully meaning to get through it — pen out, coffee there, the whole little setup — and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three completely different things. Not even useful things half the time, just… whatever my brain has wandered into. And I’ve barely taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nEven if the topic is interesting, I can still slip off like that, unless there’s something very immediate pulling me back. It’s frustrating because I’m not sitting there thinking, “I don’t care.” I do care. I just sort of lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, like you need to be moving or can’t sit still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, really. I don’t think of myself as especially physically restless, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I might shift around in a chair or get up to make tea and then notice the kitchen needs wiping down, and then remember there was something in the fridge I meant to use before it went off — sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But no, I wouldn’t say I have this strong need to be moving all the time. At least, it’s not something I’ve noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, actually. I mean, I worry about things when there’s something obvious going on, like if I’ve forgotten something or there’s a deadline — but just worrying for no reason… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nAlthough, I suppose everyone has those nights where you suddenly remember some awkward conversation from years ago, or start thinking about whether the car needs booking in, and then somehow you’re wondering if you replied to an email from last Tuesday. Sorry, that’s a bit of a ramble.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed worry as the main thing. It’s not what I’d say I’m dealing with most, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel a bit flat or fed up, but it usually feels tied to being behind on things or just being tired.\n\nActually, “down” is hard to judge, because sometimes I’ll come home from school and just sort of collapse on the sofa, and then I realize I’ve still got marking or laundry or whatever, and that makes me feel worse — but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just… normal life piling up.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down a lot recently. Yes, definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s most visible, I suppose, because there are systems and deadlines and other people notice. My department chair has actually put me on a support plan because I’ve missed follow-through on things too many times — emails, paperwork, tasks I said I’d do.

I’ll fully intend to sort something, and then it just sort of slips, and then suddenly it’s overdue or she’s had to remind me three times.\n\nAnd work-wise it’s got more serious than I wanted to admit — my department chair actually put me on a support plan because I wasn’t following through on things reliably enough. I remember sitting in that meeting thinking about how the room had those awful flickering lights, which is not the point, obviously… but yeah. It’s causing real problems, not just annoying me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing something you find genuinely interesting, are you usually able to stay focused?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, if it really grabs me I can do better… but it’s not reliable, which is kind of why I came in.\n\nLike, with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, I’ll sit down fully meaning to get through it — pen out, coffee there, the whole little setup — and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same page and thinking about three completely different things. Not even useful things half the time, just… whatever my brain has wandered into. And I’ve barely taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nEven if the topic is interesting, I can still slip off like that, unless there’s something very immediate pulling me back. It’s frustrating because I’m not sitting there thinking, “I don’t care.” I do care. I just sort of lose the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, like you need to be moving or can’t sit still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, really. I don’t think of myself as especially physically restless, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I might shift around in a chair or get up to make tea and then notice the kitchen needs wiping down, and then remember there was something in the fridge I meant to use before it went off — sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But no, I wouldn’t say I have this strong need to be moving all the time. At least, it’s not something I’ve noticed as a main problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to deal with?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, actually. I mean, I worry about things when there’s something obvious going on, like if I’ve forgotten something or there’s a deadline — but just worrying for no reason… I’m not sure I’d describe it that way.\n\nAlthough, I suppose everyone has those nights where you suddenly remember some awkward conversation from years ago, or start thinking about whether the car needs booking in, and then somehow you’re wondering if you replied to an email from last Tuesday. Sorry, that’s a bit of a ramble.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed worry as the main thing. It’s not what I’d say I’m dealing with most, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say depressed, exactly. I mean, I have days where I feel a bit flat or fed up, but it usually feels tied to being behind on things or just being tired.\n\nActually, “down” is hard to judge, because sometimes I’ll come home from school and just sort of collapse on the sofa, and then I realize I’ve still got marking or laundry or whatever, and that makes me feel worse — but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just… normal life piling up.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed feeling down a lot recently. Not as the main thing, anyway.\n\nDoctor: When your attention drifts, do you ever feel detached or as if you 'zone out' and lose track of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think “zone out” is probably the right phrase, though I don’t know if I’d call it detached exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark, or some curriculum document I genuinely mean to get through, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been looking at the same page the whole time. My eyes are on it, but I’m thinking about three other things, or sort of drifting, and I haven’t actually taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m unaware of where I am, really. It’s just… I lose the thread very easily, and then I’m annoyed because I did sit down to do it. That’s actually a big part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose or misplace things you need, like keys, papers, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. I’m constantly hunting for things I need for the day — classroom keys, lesson notes, my USB drive, that sort of thing.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’ll be sure I had something in my hand five minutes earlier, and then suddenly it’s just gone. And then I’m retracing my steps around the staff room or my classroom, checking the same pile of papers three times.\n\nIt happens with refereeing stuff as well — like the whistle or cards — and I’ll think, “Right, I definitely put them somewhere sensible,” which apparently means absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention and organization problems been fairly constant throughout your life, rather than only starting during a particular period?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It didn’t suddenly start recently or anything.\n\nI mean, even in primary school, my reports had that sort of line teachers love — “bright but careless,” “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised,” things like that. My mum still brings it up sometimes, usually when she’s making some point about me leaving a cupboard open or— sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nBut yes, it goes back a long way. My parents were always finding worksheets crumpled up in my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, and just never handed in. So it’s pretty familiar, unfortunately.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and organization problems happen in more than one setting, such as at home as well as at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely not just at work. Work is where it’s most visible, I suppose, because there are systems and deadlines and other people notice. My department chair has actually put me on a support plan because I’ve missed follow-through on things too many times — emails, paperwork, tasks I said I’d do.\n\nBut it’s at home as well. My wife has started taking over the bills and reminders, which is… not great to admit. She’ll say, “I can’t rely on you to remember this,” and she’s not wrong. I hate that, honestly, because I’m not trying to dump it on her. It’s just if something isn’t right in front of me, or written somewhere I actually look at, it seems to disappear.\n\nAnd then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like reorganising the recycling or looking for a missing charger, and meanwhile the thing that actually mattered— sorry, yes, the short answer is it happens in both places. Work and home."
  }

Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. And my parents were forever pulling these crumpled worksheets out of my backpack that I’d done, or half-done, but just never handed in. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. My eyes are on it, but I’m thinking about three other things, or sort of drifting, and I haven’t actually taken in what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not like I’m unaware of where I am, really. It’s just… I lose the thread very easily, and then I’m annoyed because I did sit down to do it. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.
